### Page 1

Economic Trends

2026 年 7 月 22 日

1/3 
2025 年スポットワーク市場規模の推計 
～2025 年のスポットワーク市場規模は 1,503 億円と推計、3 年間で約 4 倍に拡大～ 
経済調査部 主席エコノミスト 星野 卓也 
（℡：050-5474-7497）

（要旨）

（※）本稿はタイミー・スポットワーク研究所における筆者執筆レポートを基に作成 
（https://spotwork.timee.co.jp/entry/report/marketsize-2025）。

2025 年のスポットワーク市場規模は1,503 億円と推計 
今回、筆者が客員研究員を務める（株）タイミーからスポットワークサービス「タイミー」の事
業データの提供を受け、 それをもとに近年国内で広がるスポットワーク市場の規模を推計した。 推
計方法は本稿末で解説している。スポットワークとは、数時間や１日単位など短時間・単発で働く
形態であり、 スキマバイトとも呼ばれるものだ。 スマートフォンのアプリなどを介して企業と労働
者のマッチングが行われ、 近年その手軽さ、 柔軟さなどを背景に急速に広がっている労働形態であ
る。 
2025 年のスポットワーク市場規模（スポットワークで働く人に対して支払われた総賃金額）は
1,503 億円と推計される。 また、 スポットワークの延べ労働時間は12,953 万時間に達したと推計さ
れる。総賃金額、延べ労働時間ともに 2022 年対比では約 4 倍に拡大した。市場規模について各年
の成長率をみると、2023 年は＋96.0％、2024 年は＋62.5％、2025 年は＋26.7％であった。マーケ
ットの成熟が進むもとで成長率は低くなっているが、依然として高い伸びが続いているといえる。 
また、2025 年のスポットワーク延べ労働時間が国内全体の延べ労働時間 （正社員やパート・アル
バイトを含む） に占める割合は0.13％程度、 パート・アルバイト労働者の延べ労働時間との対比で
は 0.9％程度となる。こちらも年々上昇傾向にあり、労働市場においてスポットワークが存在感を
徐々に高めていることがうかがえる。 
特に、 スポットワークの中心職種である運搬 （配達や倉庫作業など） 、 接客・給仕 （飲食店やレジ
○ （株）タイミーの事業データをもとにスポットワーク市場の規模を推定。2025 年は 1,503 億
円（前年比＋26.7％）と、3 年で約4 倍に拡大。特にスポットワークの広がる「運搬の職業」
ではパート・アルバイト労働者の延べ労働時間に占める比率が8.7％に達し、 スポットワーク
が相応に存在感を高めていることがうかがえる。

### Page 2

2/3 
ャー施設での接客など） 、 商品販売 （コンビニやスーパーでの販売など） の仕事に絞って、 パート・
アルバイト労働者の延べ労働時間との対比をみると、 スポットワーク延べ労働時間はそれぞれ運搬
が8.7％、 接客・給仕が3.4％、 商品販売が1.0％となった。 スポットワークが広がっている運搬の
職業では特に高い数字となっている。 スポットワークが国内の労働市場においても相応に存在感を
高めていることがうかがえる。

業紹介実績データの公表や新たに取得した各社データを反映したもの。

経済研究所が信ずるに足ると判断した情報に基づき作成していますが、その正確性、完全性に対する責任は負いません。見通しは予告
なく変更されることがあります。また、記載された内容は、第一生命保険ないしはその関連会社の投資方針と常に整合的であるとは限
りません。
3/3
（推計方法の概要）
タイミーの業務データ （2025 年 2－4 月期～2025 年 11～2026 年１月期を 2025 年としている） から、 １年間の支給総賃
金額や延べ労働時間を抽出。 他の主要スポットワーク事業者については、 厚生労働省 「人材総合サービスサイト」 におけ
る職業紹介実績：4 カ月未満有期雇用（人日）のデータをウェイトとみなしたうえでタイミーデータから割り戻しを行っ
た（タイミー以外については案件当たりの平均賃金水準などをタイミーと同等と仮定） 。人材総合サービスサイトでデー
タが得られなかった企業については、各社の公表データやタイミーデータ等をもとにウェイトを別途推定している。
国内のマクロ経済対比での規模については、総務省「労働力調査」 、厚生労働省「毎月勤労統計」 、 「賃金構造基本統計

調査」をもとに年間の総労働投入量を計算。算出した値を推定したスポットワーク市場規模と対比した。

### Page 1

매수 (유지)  
26년 2분기. DS가 다했다  
삼성전자의 26년 2분기 잠정 매출액은 26년 1분기 대비 27.9% 증가한 171조원 
이다. 이전 전망 대비 증가한 규모이다. DS 사업부를 상향 조정했다. 26년 2분기 
영업이익은 26년 1분기 대비 56.2% 증가한 89.4조원으로 이전 전망치 대비 증가한 
규모이다. 26 년 1분기 성과급을 포함한 26년 2분기 실적에 포함된 성과급 관련 
비용은 약 17조원으로 추정한다. DS가 26년 1분기 대비 가장 큰 폭의 성장을 보인 
것으로 추정한다. DRAM, NAND ASP 가 이전 전망 대비 크게 상승한 영향이다. 
MX가 영업적자 전환한 것은 메모리 가격 상승에 따른 영향으로 추정한다.  
메모리 전성 시대 
삼성전자 실적은 DS 사업부가 주도할 것으로 예상한다. 디스플레이는 Set 판매 
부진으로 지난 해 대비 부진할 것으로 예상하고, MX/ 네트워크 사업부는 2분기 
이후로 영업적자 상황이 지속될 것으로 전망한다. VD/가전은 가전 부진으로 하반기 
영업적자가 예상된다. 메모리는 이전 전망 대비 개선된 실적을 하반기에도 이어갈 
것으로 전망한다. 가격 상승이 이전 전망 대비 높기 때문이다. 디스플레이 사업부도 
계절성으로 상반기 대비 하반기가 실적이 좋겠지만, 메모리 실적 개선 폭은 이를 
크게 상회할 전망이다. DS  사업부 영업이익이 전사 영업이익과 동일한 수준일 
것으로 추정한다.  
투자의견 매수, 목표주가 460,000원으로 상향  
삼성전자에 대한 투자의견은 매수를 유지한다. AI 생태계에서 메모리의 입지가 
커져가는 상황에서 삼성전자는 입지는 더욱 강화될 것으로 예상하고, 여전히 저평가 
국면에 있다고 판단하기 때문이다. HBM 은 HBM4 물량이 본격화되는 26년 
3분기부터 외형 성장에 기여할 것으로 기대한다. 목표주가는 2026년 예상 BPS 
114,449원에 PBR 4.0배를 적용한 460,000원으로 31.4% 상향 조정한다.

Compliance Notice 
동 자료에 게재된 내용들은 외부의 압력이나 부당한 간섭없이 본인의 의견을 정확하게 반영하여 작성되었음을 확인합니다. 
동 자료는 기관투자가 또는 제3자에게 사전 제공한 사실이 없습니다. 
동 자료는 조사분석자료 작성에 참여한 외부인(계열회사 및 그 임직원등)이 없습니다. 
조사분석 담당자 및 배우자는 해당종목과 재산적 이해관계가 없습니다. 
동 자료에 언급된 종목의 지분율 1%이상 보유하고 있지 않습니다. 
당사는 상기 명시한 사항 외 고지해야 하는 특별한 이해관계가 없습니다.

### Page 1

두산에너빌리티 (034020)

EARNINGS REVIEW Company Analysis | 건설/원전 | 2026. 7. 28  
채워가는 곳간

2Q26 Review: 두산밥캣 일회성 이익 반영으로 호실적 
두산에너빌리티 2분기 매출액은 4조 7,248억원으로 전년동기대비 3.4% 증가, 컨센
서스에 비교적 부합했다. 반면 영업이익은 3,143억원으로 전년동기대비 15.9% 증가, 
컨센서스 영업이익 2,669억원 대비 17.7% 상회하는 호실적을 기록했다. 두산퓨얼셀
이 주기기 매출 공백과 스택 교체비용 등의 일회성 비용 반영에 따라 QoQ 적자 폭을 
확대했음에도 불구하고, 1) 두산밥캣에서 대미 상호관세 환급효과가 크게 반영되며 이
를 상쇄했고, 2) 에너빌리티부문에서 안정적인 실적 성장이 나타난 점이 금번 호실적의 
주요 요인이다. 상반기 신규수주는 대형 원전 EPC 수주 지연에도 가이던스 13.3조원 
중 53.4%인 7.1조원을 달성하며 성장을 위한 곳간을 차곡차곡 채우고 있다. 
시간이 갈수록 더해져만 가는 기회들 
두산에너빌리티에 대해 투자의견 Buy 유지, 목표주가를 120,000원으로 하향하나 여
전히 건설/원전주 내 Top picks 종목으로 추천을 유지하겠다. 목표 EBITDA는 2035
년 EBITDA에 WACC 8%를 적용, 목표 EV/EBITDA는 Global Peers인 GE Vernova
의 2026년 EV/EBITDA 멀티플에서 자회사 실적 변동성을 고려해 10% 할인 적용했
다. 두산에너빌리티의 투자 포인트와 성장 방향성은 가스, 원전으로 명확하다. 1) 글로
벌 전력 수요 증가로 대형 가스 터빈 수급 쇼티지는 당분간 지속될 전망이다. 2) AI 데
이터센터 전력수요 급증이 안정적 기저발전인 대형 원전, SMR의 수요를 높이며 핵심 
기자재 발주가 늘어날 것으로 보인다. 이에 더해 한국 정부의 대미투자로 논의될 것으
로 예상되는 에너지 사업, 미국 정부의 AP1000 사업 가속화를 위한 금융지원, 우리 
나라 서남권 메가프로젝트 발표로 인한 국내 전력수요 증가에 따른 가스, 원전 발주 풀 
확대 등 시간이 지날수록 알파 모멘텀이 더욱 더해지고 있다. 최근 주가는 시장 조정에 
따라 차익실현 매물 출회되며 다소 부진했으나, 명확한 수주 성과와 시장 파이 확대를 
감안하면 다시금 사지 않을 이유가 없다는 판단이다. 
 Analyst 김세련 
sally.kim@ls-sec.co.kr 
Buy (유지)

Document 212: AI-carus_ (2026-07-29)

Source PDF: all report pdf/AI-carus_ (2026-07-29).pdf

### Page 1

#australia #luciellis #inflation #technology
AI-carus?
By Luci Ellis
Chief Economist Westpac Group, Westpac
08:31 July 17 2026 Share
AI is flying high. Will it fly too close to the sun and crash like Icarus in Greek myth? Physical
constraints and ROI are the sunrays in this story. The likely downswing in prices and
investment could be challenging but need not end in meltdown.
Is AI a bubble? The extraordinary valuations and scale of investment raise the question. Missing,

though, is the speculative behaviour of buying an asset now with the intent of resale for profit later.

### Page 2

The exceptional valuations on AI-adjacent firms are matched only by the extraordinary scale of capital
expenditure on data centres, both overseas and in Australia. The use of leverage and sometimes
opaque financing structures twitch the antennae of those of us who have been through financial boom–
bust cycles before. Naturally, people question the return on investment and whether this is a “bubble”.
But is it?
It helps to define terms. The usual definition of a bubble is a deviation of asset prices from

“fundamental” value, but this is only as good as your model of fundamentals. The distinctive feature in
episodes labelled as bubbles is speculative behaviour: investors buying an asset mainly or solely
because they expect its price will rise so they can resell at a profit. (In technical terms, the current price
of the asset depends on expectations of future price growth). In the extreme, the market is in “Greater
Fool” territory, where investors admit that they do not think the revenue warrants the price, but believe

they will find a Greater Fool to buy it later.
Property assets are one of the main areas where “bubbly” boom–bust cycles can occur, because
investors can use more leverage than for other assets. The asset also does not become obsolete
quickly and much of the value is in the non-depreciating land or location component.
Data centres are a kind of property, and leverage is clearly being used to finance their construction.
Even so, the speculative behaviour that typically characterises property bubbles is missing: nobody is

making these investments in expectation of reselling data centres to make a quick buck. It is all about
the expected value of the computing services these data centres will provide. Currently the “cost of
compute” seems to be on the rise as the firms building the models try to shift their customer bases
away from free and flat-rate subscriptions onto higher-cost pay-by-use plans. Yet everyone knows that
AI can only achieve the desired returns by expanding usage, which means it needs to be cheaper than

the human labour it seeks to replace. Even if investment decisions are based on expected returns on
services provided by that capital, the realism of those return expectations can and should be
scrutinised.
It is natural to want to look for parallels in history.
The seemingly obvious one is the ‘dot com’ boom of the late 1990s/early 2000s. But while there are
some similarities to that episode, there are also important differences. Back then, the tech firms

building the new technology were not so financially intertwined with the telco firms buying the
It is natural to want to look for parallels in history. Neither the late 1990s/early 2000s ‘dot com’ nor the
19th century railway booms quite match the current episode. Better analogies may be found in other
price cycles stemming from investment booms, spurred by structural shifts. In the rush to build for the
new technology, construction costs and end-product prices rise before eventually correcting. Time-to-

build and resource constraints can create price cycles even where classic speculative behaviour is
absent.
Non-financial goals around geopolitics or a rush to build a god-like intelligence could blind investors
to commercial reality and encourage investments that do not pay off. To avoid a mad flap that takes
us, Icarus-like, too close to the sun, choices must be based on cost and ROI.

### Page 3

spectrum, laying the fibre – and borrowing the money.
There are also similarities to the railway boom of the 19th century, except that location is not as
inherent to a data centre. You can train an LLM anywhere you can access the electricity, water and
construction inputs. A railway, by contrast, goes from somewhere to somewhere else, and the returns
to duplicating the route decline rapidly. Put more technically, data centres do not have the same

network effects or location-based natural monopoly characteristics.
Better analogies to the current AI/data centre boom can be found in other price cycles. Many boom–
bust cycles start from something real and substantial. A new technology might instigate an equities
boom. Opening up a new region might instigate a land boom there. The value underlying the boom
remains even after the period of over-exuberance ends. We did not regret creating the Internet, or

building that fibre network, or opening up regions through better transport.
What we did sometimes do, though, is pay too much for what we got. If everyone is rushing to build the
infrastructure, hoping not to be the Slower Builder who ends up losing money, pressure on construction
resources rises, and so do construction prices. We have seen similar cost surges during the mining
boom and more recently in the cycle of infrastructure projects in the eastern seaboard states. (This is

likely one reason why suppliers of construction materials were so quick to pass increased fuel and
transport costs into their prices.)
Along the way, the cost of the services also rise, precisely because of the lags involved in expanding
the infrastructure. There is an analogy here in things like shipping costs, which show boom–bust price
cycles even though there has obviously never been a bubble in shipping services. In shipping, office
property and now data centres, demand might increase but supply is fixed in the short term because it

takes time to build that infrastructure. The price of the service therefore rises in the upswing phase.
This upswing phase can also spill over into related sectors, as seen this time in the broader tech
sector. Export prices for Korean semiconductors have exploded in the past 6–9 months, up more than
150% over the year. Pass-through to US import prices is more muted, given lags and the usual pricing
to market, but tech inflation has increased. As well as the much-publicised price increases for iPads

and Macs, the CPI components covering computers and other tech goods have picked up recently in
Australia and other advanced economies, to inflation rates close to the peak of the pandemic. This is a
departure from their usual deflationary pulse and shows that the current inflationary environment is
about more than the US–Iran conflict.
The high costs of the upswing phase nonetheless contain the seeds of their reversal. They discourage
use of the underlying service, so adoption is slower than its boosters assume. They also encourage

further innovations to economise on the expensive input; routing algorithms to choose the most cost-
effective AI model are one example. Over time, as data centres are completed, the supply of computing
power expands and its price falls. Investments that only made sense when the service’s price was high
will become distressed.
What worries me about the current boom is how far financial considerations seem to sit in the
background. Capitalism has its drawbacks, but at least raw commercial incentives limit people’s

### Page 4

Related articles
appetite to overpay for things. When all the focus is instead on geopolitical rivalries or being the first to
build god-like intelligence, commercial discipline is lost. A potentially beneficial innovation wave instead
risks becoming an Icarus-like mad flap that takes us too close to the sun. That is the perfect
environment for a price cycle, including in the tech sector more broadly.
The end result need not be a meltdown. That depends on the debt: how much there is relative to

revenues after the price downswing, who borrowed it and who lent it. The role of deep-pocketed tech
firms eases some concerns. Some borrowers are substantial firms with other revenues. In addition,
other tech firms who have not leveraged themselves to the data centre build-out have big enough cash
piles to pick up the pieces (at discounted prices) should some projects come unstuck. Regulators will
be most concerned about whether the lenders who lent to the hyperscalers and AI firms in turn funded

themselves from parts of the financial system that amplify shocks through their leverage and
connection to the regulated banking system.
The answer is not to regulate AI out of existence or stymie its further development – a point the federal
government has clearly heeded in the PM’s latest announcements. The advice I’d give now is the same
as I gave a decade ago, in a different context: in the face of structural change giving rise to a boom, go
in with your eyes open. Don’t get ahead of yourself. And have something to catch you when you fall.

Humans have managed to transcend Greek mythology and master the art of flying without melting. Yet
the message of Icarus’s story endures: there are dangers when hubris goes unchecked. To avoid
getting ahead of yourself, it helps to let commercial considerations shape decisions, especially around
borrowing. Far better that than basing decisions on imagined scenarios that do not survive contact with
real-world economics.

1――信頼される日本、影響力を持つ中国

高市政権は発足後、東南アジア各国との首脳外交を重ね、ASEAN との関係を重視する姿勢を示し
ている。では、ASEAN 側から日本はどのようにみられているのだろうか。 
 ISEAS ユソフ・イシャク研究所が2026 年 4 月に公表した調査によると、日本を「信頼する」と
した回答は65.6％に上り、 欧州連合 （EU）の55.9％、 米国の44.0％、 中国の39.8％、 インドの38.5％
を上回り、主要国・地域のなかで最も高かった。 
国別にみると、 日本を信頼する割合はフィリピンが77.3％と最も高く、 ラオスが76.7％、 ブルネイ
が 72.9％、 カンボジアが72.0％と続いた。 一方、 インドネシアは47.9％と最も低く、 シンガポールは
51.6％、マレーシアは53.0％にとどまった（図表１） 。日本はASEAN 全体で高い信頼を得ているも
のの、その程度には国ごとの差もみられる。 
日本を信頼する理由としては、 「国際法を尊重し、 擁護する責任あるステークホルダー」 であること
を挙げる回答が41.4％と最も多かった。 長年にわたる開発協力やインフラ整備、 企業進出などを通じ
て、一貫性のあるパートナーとして受け止められてきたことが、評価の背景にあるとみられる。 
もっとも、 信頼の高さが、 そのまま地域における経済的な影響力の大きさを意味するわけではない。

電子部品、素材などの供給拡大も伴っている。米国の対中関税引き上げを背景に、企業が中国に置い
ていた生産工程の一部を東南アジアへ移しても、移転先の工場が中国製の設備や中間財を調達する場

き下げる動きと捉えられてきたが、 実際には中国とASEAN をまたぐ生産ネットワークの拡大につな

Document 255: Editorial _ Emerging economies_ why is the energy shock not derailing growth driven by the AI boom_ (2026-07-20)

EMERGING ECONOMIES: WHY IS THE ENERGY SHOCK NOT DERAILING GROWTH DRIVEN BY THE AI BOOM?
Despite the war in Iran, the closure of the Strait of Hormuz and the temporary surge in energy prices, emerging eco-
nomies have so far avoided a crisis scenario. Their growth is slowing marginally, inflation remains contained in most
countries and financial markets have not collapsed. The most powerful growth engine is coming from Asia: global

demand for chips, data centers and electronic goods linked to artificial intelligence is offsetting part of the oil shock
and reshaping the external balances of several emerging countries.
1 BNP Paribas, 16 June 2026: Reopening of the Strait of Hormuz: the oil market between short-term relief and persisting uncertainties
Emerging economies have so far withstood the energy shock caused
by the conflict in the Middle East better than expected. The surge in

oil, gas and energy-related input prices was rapid, but less inflatio -
nary than in 2022. If monetary easing cycles have been interrupted in
many countries, most central banks have been able to keep their rates
unchanged over the past four months. Emerging financial markets did
not experience a generalized loss of confidence, while macroeconomic
buffers stronger than in summer 2022 helped absorb the rise in energy
costs. In Asia, the region most dependent on Middle Eastern hydro -

carbons, authorities have limited the risks of shortages by diversifying
supply sources, mobilizing reserves and adjusting demand. Most im -
portantly, countries exporting technological goods have been benefiting
strongly from the boom in artificial intelligence. Investments in AI phy-
sical infrastructure and global demand for chips and other electronic
goods have been supporting growth and external accounts of several
emerging economies, sometimes more than the energy shock pena -

lizes them. In the short term, the average growth of emerging markets
should therefore slow down only moderately. Risks remain high, howe-
ver, including more persistent inflation, possible increases in US Fed
rates, geopolitical tensions, volatility in commodity prices and the risk
of a tech cycle correction.
GROWTH IN EMERGING ECONOMIES: THE ENERGY SHOCK SLOWS ACTI-
VITY AND ACCELERATES INFLATION, WITHOUT CAUSING A CRISIS
Emerging market economies have overall withstood the energy shock

caused by the war in Iran. Their average growth, solid in the first quar-
ter of 2026 (estimated at +1.0% q/q), is expected to slow only very mo-
derately in the second quarter. Neither business surveys nor activity
indicators experienced significant deterioration during the spring. The
aggregate manufacturing PMI for emerging countries fell in March
before rebounding in April and remaining almost stable thereafter;
in June, it was in expansion territory (at 51.4) and slightly above its

January-February average level. By the end of June, only four of the
eighteen major emerging economies (Chile, Indonesia, Türkiye, Egypt)
had a manufacturing PMI below 50 and lower than in February. In in -
dustrialized Asian countries that are also net hydrocarbon importers,
PMIs remained well-oriented despite the energy shock. In Vietnam, real
GDP growth accelerated to +8.4% y/y in Q2, up from +7.9% in Q1, driven
by strong industrial production and export performance. In China, eco-

nomic growth slowed in Q2 (+4.3% y/y after +5% in Q1), notably curbed
by rising energy prices, lower public spending and weak demand of the
domestic private sector (see the note on China, page 10).
On the inflation front, the shock proved less severe than expected:
inflationary pressures have emerged quickly but remained moderate.
The closure of the Strait of Hormuz had an immediate and significant
impact on global energy prices (Brent price: +57% between late Fe -

bruary and late April – see table A & chart B, page 6), as well as on
fertilizer and petrochemical product prices. The shock quickly affected
fuel prices and transport costs around the world.
However, on the one hand, the increases have so far spread little to
agricultural and food prices. This has significantly limited the impact
of the energy shock on CPI inflation (energy typically accounts for less
than 15% of the CPI basket in emerging countries, while the average

weight of food is 30%), particularly when compared to the 2022 shock
resulting from the war in Ukraine. On the other hand, oil prices fell from
late April (Brent price: -19% in May and -21% in June), helped by the
faster reduction in OECD countries’ stocks and lower Chinese oil im -
ports, and then by prospects of the reopening of the Strait of Hormuz1.
The median CPI inflation rate in emerging markets has risen
moderately, from 3.2% y/y in February to 4.3% in June. Inflation thus

remains contained in a majority of countries (see chart C, page 6).
In the countries most severely affected (notably the Philippines and
Thailand, followed by Kenya, South Africa and Bulgaria), the inflationary
shock is explained by a greater direct exposure to the energy price
shock (Chart 1, above) and, in some cases, by the depreciation of their
currency. Conversely, inflationary pressures have been the lowest in
countries using subsidies or fuel price controls (for example India,

Indonesia, Malaysia or the Gulf countries). In China, rising energy prices
have helped reduce deflationary pressures (though not addressing the
underlying structural causes): producer prices have been increasing
since March after more than three years of decline, and CPI inflation
has accelerated slightly to +1.1% y/y in Q2 2026 vs. 0.8% in Q1.
Monetary easing cycles, which were underway in many countries at
the beginning of the year, have generally been interrupted. But interest

rate hikes have been few and limited in scope (see chart D, page 6).
Since the beginning of the war in Iran, only a few central banks have
significantly raised their policy rates, but not necessarily in response to
the energy shock. It is, in particular, the case of Indonesia and Colombia:

while their direct exposure to the energy price shock is limited
(Chart 1, page 3), monetary authorities reacted, in the first case, to the

in the second, to the expansionary drift of fiscal policy and its effects on
inflation (cf. Indonesia & Colombia, pages 16 and 26). Monetary policy

was also tightened in the Philippines and, to a lesser extent, in South
Africa and the Czech Republic, as central banks acted quickly when
inflation moved away from target (3%±1 for the Philippines and South
Africa, 2%±1 for the Czech Republic). Korea’s central bank is the latest
to have raised its policy rate on 16 July (+25bp) due to inflationary
pressures in a context of solid economic growth (cf. South Korea,
page 12).
A large number of central banks have kept their key interest rates un-

changed, including in Asia. The monetary status quo could be main -
tained in Thailand (which is emerging from a year of deflation), Viet -
nam (whose central bank has objectives to stabilize prices but also to
support demand) and China (which is seeking to stimulate credit and
combat deflationary pressures). Brazil and Hungary are two exceptions
as they have, as planned before the war, started their monetary easing
cycle (since February, policy rates have been cut by 75bp in Brazil and

50bp in Hungary).
WHY ARE EMERGING COUNTRIES COPING BETTER THAN IN 2022 WITH
THE ENERGY SHOCK?
Macroeconomic resilience. Emerging countries’ macroeconomic fun -
damentals and their cyclical position have helped cushion the impact
of the external shock. At the beginning of the year, emerging economies
as a whole, experienced dynamics of strong growth, disinflation, and
lowering public and external account imbalances. In India, for example,
the authorities have more leeway to support the economy thanks to a

smaller budget imbalance, lower current account deficit and more mo-
derate inflation than in 2022 (cf. India, page 14). In Egypt, the adjust -
ment of economic policies over the last few quarters (fiscal reforms,
increased exchange rate flexibility) and support from international cre-
ditors have been helping to limit the destabilizing effect of rising ener-
gy prices (cf. Egypt, page 32). In Poland and more generally in Central
Europe, inflation is much more moderate than in 2022, and inflation

expectations are better anchored (cf. Poland, page 18).
Energy resilience. The supply shock was less severe than feared at the
time of the first military attacks in late February. Hydrocarbon shor -
tages and their direct effects on activity have been more limited. Asian
countries – despite being the most exposed to supply disruption risks
due to their reliance on hydrocarbon imports from the Middle East –
have demonstrated strong adaptive capacity. They rapidly implemented

solutions to reduce the risk of shortages, with measures to contain de-
mand, search of alternative supply sources (for instance from Russia,
floating stocks, etc.). Central European2 and Latin American countries3
are less or little dependent on hydrocarbons from the Middle East.
The absence of a financial shock. Emerging financial markets have
not experienced widespread stress since the beginning of the war in
Iran. Investment flows have been highly volatile in an international

context marked by rising geopolitical risks and upheavals linked to the
AI boom. However, overall, emerging markets have not experienced any
major loss of foreign investor confidence. Financial conditions have
tightened only slightly for emerging sovereign and private borrowers.
2 BNP Paribas, 27 April 2026: Energy shock: The four keys to Central Europe’s resilience
3 BNP Paribas, 15 April 2026: Latin America: more exposed to inflationary pressures than to disruptions in hydrocarbon supplies

4 BNP Paribas, 6 March 2026: The rise of artificial intelligence: strategic opportunities for emerging countries
5 Growth in the volume of world merchandise exports accelerated from 2.8% in 2024 (1.8% in value) to 4.4% in 2025 (6.8% in value) and 6.7% y/y in the first four months of 2026
(CPB data), largely driven by demand for AI-related goods.
6 According to Oxford Economics estimates, Taiwan benefits from the most significant positive impact on its GDP and net exports from a USD100 increase in US imports of

AI-related goods. Vietnam, Malaysia, South Korea, Thailand and China follow. Cf. Oxford Economics, 21 May 2026: How AI is shaping regional supply chains – and fuelling a new
savings glut.
CDS spreads and domestic bond yields tensions have remained
contained and lower than in 2022, and currencies have generally held
up well (Chart E, page 6). Between 27 February and 18 June 2026 (me-
morandum of understanding between the United States and Iran), the

most significant depreciations affected either the countries most di -
rectly exposed to the energy shock, such as Thailand (-5.3% vs. USD),
South Korea (-6.2%) or the Philippines (-4.5%), or those with specific
macroeconomic vulnerabilities, such as Indonesia (-5.7% vs. USD), Ro-
mania (-5.4%) and Türkiye (-5.4%). Emerging currencies appreciated
between the signing of the MOU and the resumption of military attacks
in mid-July.
Another factor contributing to the broad resilience of the emerging eco-

nomies is the boom in AI and global demand for AI-related goods.
THE EXPANSION OF AI INFRASTRUCTURE SUPPORTS ACTIVITY IN ASIA
For emerging countries as a whole, the rise of AI represents a positive
growth shock that offsets the negative impact of the energy shock.
So far, the impact of AI expansion on emerging economies has been
mainly through the spillover effects of investments in the physical in -
frastructure of AI 4, via supply chains and global trade 5. The countries

that produce the goods necessary for the construction of data centers
and other AI infrastructures (critical metals and, above all, chips and
other electronic goods) have indeed a strategic advantage in their in -
ternational relations, a solid export base and a growth driver. In some
of these countries, the surge in global demand for AI-related goods is
also reshaping external account dynamics.
Countries that produce critical minerals hold a particularly strategic

position, but economic growth gains directly linked to the AI boom are
modest in the short term. They export these minerals largely in their
raw state, without great added value, and the quantities used are li -
mited. While all AI-related goods account for about 15% of the total va-
lue of global exports (Oxford Economics data), raw materials represent
less than 3%. However, the rise in critical metal prices has supported
the terms of trade for exporting countries (for example, the price of

copper has increased by 42% over the past year and by 8% since the
beginning of 2026). This has enabled, particularly in certain Latin Ame-
rican countries, to offset the impact of rising prices of imported hydro-
carbons since the beginning of the conflict in the Middle East.
Countries that produce chips and other electronic goods have, on the
contrary, benefited from strong support for activity and exports over
the last few quarters. It essentially concerns Asian countries, which

provide 65% of global exports of AI-related goods and 85% of semi -
conductor exports (2025 data). Taiwan, as well as South Korea, China,
Vietnam, Malaysia and Thailand are the main beneficiaries of the rise
in global demand for AI-related goods in terms of impact on activity 6.
On the other hand, the impact on current account balances varies
widely and depends on the producing country’s position in the value
chains.
Upstream in the chain, Taiwan and South Korea produce the most

sophisticated and indispensable chips for AI, and the rise of AI supports
activity and current account surpluses. Their merchandise exports
have shown very high growth rates (+47% y/y in value in both Korea
and Taiwan in H1 2026), driven by a strong increase in both volume
(estimated at over 20% y/y in H1) and prices (see chart F, page 6).
Export prices are supported by the rapid increase in demand and the
rise in the value chain (in South Korea, the average price of exported

### Page 3

5
EcoPerspectives Emerging Economies / July 2026 economic-research.bnpparibas.com
The bank
for a changing
world
semiconductors has more than doubled in a year). The growth in
volumes has recently slowed down, with the manufacturing sector
activity being constrained, on the one hand, by rising energy and
commodity costs and, on the other hand, by high production capacity
utilisation rates.
Boosted by production and exports of AI-related goods, Taiwan’s eco -

nomic growth reached 14.5% y/y in Q1 2026 (with a contribution from
net exports of 10.3pp) and South Korean growth accelerated to 3.8%
(with a contribution from net exports of 1.5pp). On the external account
side, strong growth in chip exports and the improvement of terms of
trade have had a direct and significant positive impact on the trade and
current account balances (cf. chart 2). External surpluses are reaching
record highs despite the rise in the energy bill. In Q1 2026, Taiwan’s

current account surplus is estimated at nearly 25% of GDP (vs. an ave-
rage of 15% over the past five years), and South Korea’s at nearly 15%
of GDP , compared to 4% over the past five years (South Korea).
China exports a wider range of AI-related goods, but their production
requires importing microprocessors and other high-value-added com-
ponents, notably from South Korea and Taiwan. The boom in AI-related
global demand is boosting export activity, and China’s AI sector is be -

coming an essential driver of growth in both the industrial and services
sectors. However, the balance of trade in AI-related goods is close to
equilibrium, as imports are increasing in line with exports. Therefore,
the boom in trade of AI-related goods does not increase China’s trade
surplus (cf. China, page 10).
Other exporters of tech goods are more specialized in the assembly,
testing and packaging of chips to form electronic components at va -

rious stages of the value chain. They are all benefiting from solid sup-
port for economic growth and export revenues. However, not all have
a position that allows them to increase their trade surpluses thanks to
the rise in global demand for AI-related goods (cf. chart 2). Malaysia
currently benefits from its status as a net exporter of hydrocarbons and
semiconductors.
At the other end of the spectrum, Thailand, Vietnam and, to a much
lesser extent, Mexico produce lower value-added goods. They are cur-

rently facing both the rise in energy costs and the rise in the price
of electronic inputs that they have to import to run their factories
(cf. Mexico, page 28). In Thailand and Vietnam, the trade balance mo-
ved from a surplus in H1 2025 to a deficit in the same period in 2026.
The boom in AI therefore tends to exacerbate their external vulnerabi-
lity. While Thailand has sufficient foreign exchange reserves to absorb
shocks to its balance of payments, Vietnam has low forex reserves and

could face some pressure on its currency in the coming months.
FORECASTS FOR 2026: ECONOMIC GROWTH CLOSE TO 4%, BUT THREE
MAJOR SOURCES OF RISK FOR TURBULENCE
Average growth in emerging economies is therefore expected to slow
only moderately in the short term. In our central scenario, we expect
average growth slightly below 4% for the whole of 2026, after 4.5%
in 2025. The slowdown is, unsurprisingly, most pronounced in the
Gulf7 and the Middle East. It is widespread but relatively moderate in

Asian countries, with the effects of the AI boom helping to offset the
consequences of the energy shock in the most industrialized econo -
mies. Asia’s average economic growth will remain the strongest among
emerging regions. The slowdown is much less pronounced in Latin
America, particularly thanks to the expected rebound in Mexico and
the strong performance of Brazilian growth (cf. Mexico and Brazil, see
pages 28 and 24), and in Central Europe, where activity is notably sup-

ported by public investment (see Regional Overviews, page 8).
7 BNP Paribas, 15 July 2026: The conflict in the Middle East: a massive blow to growth in the Gulf
The first downside risk to this central scenario is the inflationary risk.
Inflationary pressures are expected to persist, driven by durably higher
commodity prices. They could lead to a broader tightening of monetary
policies. After the signing, in late June, of the MOU between the United

States and Iran, global oil prices had returned to their pre-war levels,
but they have been increasing again since the resumption of military
attacks. They should remain volatile and high in the short term. Glo -
bal prices of critical minerals are expected to remain high, particular -
ly due to the wave of AI investments and electrification. Agricultural
commodity prices could also rise faster than expected due to a com -
bination of higher fertilizer prices and weather phenomena (El Niño,

heatwaves, unfavorable monsoon in India, etc.). The IMF expects an
18.6% increase in the average price of non-fuel commodities in 2026,
after +10% in 2025.
The second risk to our forecasts is the risk of tighter global financing
conditions for emerging borrowers, which will partly result from the
expected increase in US Fed rates and could further intensify if forei -
gn investor sentiment deteriorates. Large depreciations of emerging
currencies would then also increase inflationary pressures. In a global

context marked by geopolitical tensions and upheavals driven by the AI
boom, the risks of financial turbulence are significant.
The third risk is the potential correction of the technology cycle. A sud-
den slowdown in investments in data centers and digital infrastruc -
tures would directly weigh on economies most exposed to semiconduc-
tor value chains. Given the current narrow base of global growth and
its concentration around tech, such a correction would not be limited

to financial markets: it would also affect the real economy, particular-
ly in the industrialized countries of Asia and countries dependent on
US demand.
Article completed on 16 July 2026

Document 269: First Impressions_ NZIER Quarterly Survey of Business Opinion, June quarter 2026 (2026-07-29)

#businessconditions#newzealand
First Impressions: NZIER Quarterly Survey of Business Opinion,June quarter 2026
By Michael Gordon
Senior Economist, Westpac NZ
08:10 July 14 2026 Share
Business confidence picked up a bit in the June quarter, but Middle East tensions and
higher fuel prices remained on firms’ minds.
Key results (seasonally adjusted)
General business situation: +12 (Prev: +1)

### Page 2

Trading activity, past three months: +1 (Prev: 0) 
Trading activity, next three months: +10 (Prev: +13) 
Average selling prices, past three months: +37 (Prev: +23) 
Average selling prices, next three months: +52 (Prev: +43)

The NZIER’s June quarter survey of business opinion showed a modest lift in confidence as the
tensions in the Middle East eased. Sentiment about the general business situation rose to a net 12%
positive, compared to a net 1% in the March survey. However, the own-activity measures, which tend to
have a closer correspondence with GDP growth, were little changed for the quarter. 
As in the March quarter, the fast-moving events in the Middle East meant that the averages in this
survey hide a lot. While the survey period ran from 10 June to 7 July, nearly all of the responses were
received on two dates: on the 10th when the survey was sent out, and on the 17th after the signing of
the Memorandum of Understanding between the US and Iran. For general business sentiment, early
responses were a net 5% negative, while the second batch were a net 20% positive. With the renewed
hostilities this month, the first batch of responses is probably more representative of where sentiment
would stand if the survey was re-run today. 
Other activity indicators were mixed. Investment and hiring intentions were less negative than in the
March quarter, while profitability was expected to worsen further. 
Conditions remain mixed across industries. Manufacturing remains relatively upbeat, buoyed by strong
export sales, but respondents were more negative about the quarter ahead and noted a particularly
large increases in their costs and sale prices. The building industry was a little more positive this
quarter, but reported that they have continued to cut their prices despite soaring costs. Merchants
(wholesalers and retailers) remained positive, but less so than last quarter. 
The rise in fuel prices since March was clearly reflected in the pricing measures of the survey. A net
41% of firms reported raising their prices in the last quarter, up from 22% last quarter the highest
reading since September 2023. In the next three months, a net 54% of firms intend to raise their prices,
the highest since March 2023. 
For the RBNZ, the implications of the survey are unclear. While it’s not surprising that a number of firms
would be looking to pass on the rise in fuel and other costs, the short horizon for the survey questions
(three months ahead) means that it doesn’t tell us a lot about the potential for second-round inflationary
effects. And while the activity measures improved in some places, they don’t really seem to support the
RBNZ’s assertion that the economy has been running stronger than they expected in their May MPS
forecasts. 
Michael Gordon, Senior Economist

FRENCH CORPORATES: PEAKS IN CREATIONS PUT PEAKS IN BANKRUPTCIES INTO PERSPECTIVE
Thomas Humblot
Peaks in corporate creations, driven notably by the rise of
auto-entrepreneurs since 2009, are followed by peaks in
bankruptcies after 24 to 36 months

SOURCE: BANQUE DE FRANCE, INSEE, BNP PARIBAS
In June 2026, corporate creations cumulated over one year remained dynamic, reaching a historic peak according to INSEE, with
1,233,123 corporates set up, representing a 11.1% year-on-year increase. This momentum is not a recent phenomenon, as corporate

creations have doubled over the last decade. During this same period, the rise in bankruptcies has been ten times lower, which serves to
put current levels (approximately 70,000 bankruptcies cumulated over one year in March 2026) into perspective. Historically, the peaks
and troughs in corporate bankruptcies are following those of corporate creations, with an average lag of 24 to 36 months. Therefore, the
absolute number of bankruptcies provides an incomplete reflection of the financial health of the French production base, and must be

supplemented by other indicators.

Corporate creations by type

Corporate creations have doubled in ten years, but bankruptcies have not. Over
the past decade, the number of corporates created annually has doubled, as
creations were plateauing at around 570,000 per year between 2012 and 2015,

while they reached 1,166,000 in 2025 (+104%). During these same two periods,
bankruptcies cumulated over one year increased by only 11%. The introduction
of the ‘auto-entrepreneur’ status on 1st January 20091, followed by the doubling
of the turnover ceiling (and the explosion of delivery platforms) in 2018, largely
fuelled the rise in corporate creations during this period.
Historically, bankruptcies follow creations after 24 to 36 months. As a result,

the peaks seen since March 2026 (around 70,000 bankruptcies cumulated over
one year) reflect the increase in the number of young corporates, rather than
solely the impact of successive macroeconomic shocks. Given that the average
survival rate for all corporates is 75% at 3 years, compared to 65% at 5 years
(and 50% at 9 years)2, the cycles of rising and falling bankruptcies are generally
following cycles of rising and falling corporate creations after a period of 24 to

36 months3.
The financial health of corporates must be assessed beyond mere creation
and bankruptcy figures. First, bankruptcy statistics underestimate the
difficulties faced by micro-enterprises. Indeed, Banque de France statistics
only record companies undergoing receivership (‘redressement judiciaire’) or
liquidation (‘liquidation judiciaire’). However, micro-enterprises are more likely
to cease operations when facing difficulties without entering formal bankruptcy

proceedings. Therefore, bankruptcy data must be supplemented by other
indicators, such as corporate deregistrations (‘radiations’) 4, which are rising,
according to Infogreffe 5. Second, as micro-enterprises are over-represented
among bankruptcies — accounting for 92% of the total in May 2026 — the
absolute number of bankruptcies should be contextualised by other metrics.
These include the economic weight of bankrupt corporates, which allows for
weighting by a proxy for corporate size, or the ratio of non-performing loans

(NPLs) in the banking sector 6. These data confirm that there is no particularly
alarming trend in recent bankruptcy figures.
We will shortly publish a dashboard providing a synoptic overview of these
aspects.

remaining.

4 Which includes a substantial number of takeovers, company mergers and legal restructuring
of business groups without interruption to business activity.

Document 284: Is China’s Surplus Really Shrinking_ (2026-07-27)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Is China’s Surplus Really Shrinking_ (2026-07-27).pdf
Report source: CFR (Follow the Money)
Date: 2026-07-27
Pages: 11
Status: ok

### Page 1

1
EXPERT TAKE FOLLOW THE MONEY
Is China’s Surplus Really Shrinking?
Look past the gold imports, and China’s trade surplus is still climbing.
PUBLISHED
July 27, 2026 1:49 p.m.
Articles Follow the Money Share
Brad W. Setser CFR EXPERT
Whitney Shepardson Senior Fellow
The Economist is now arguing that China’s trade
surplus has peaked.
That is a strange argument, as the “real” (price-
adjusted) trade surplus is still growing. Net exports
have contributed positively to China’s growth in Q1

and Q2.*
But it also isn’t an argument that stands up too much
scrutiny. It goes away with a single adjustment.
Chip prices are up, and China imports a ton of chips.
But that isn’t in fact the needed adjustment. China’s
Log In
Regions Topics Research Experts Events The Future of American Strategy

### Page 2

2
exports of chips are up almost as much as its imports.
China imports a lot of oil but that too isn’t the needed
adjustment. The price of oil imports is up but import
volumes have famously collapsed; the commodity
import bill is up, but only modestly.
The critical adjustment is to net out imports of gold
—year-to-date imports in 2026 are $146 billion,
nearly $100 billion above the 2025 H1 total. In q2,
gold imports were an incredible 1.5 pp of GDP, so

the gold deficit now exceeds
Without that import surge, the overall goods trade
surplus would be up by over $80 billion in the first
half of 2026.
Yes, there are changes in the commodity balance and
the manufacturing balance too. See the “raw”
numbers from China’s customs, which haven’t been
adjusted for gold.

### Page 3

3
But gold is the critical adjustment to the headline
number, not chips or oil (to my surprise to be
honest).** The surge in imports, over a period when
gold prices are down, has been massive.
Facts are pesky things.

### Page 4

4
The underlying Chinese surplus is still heading up,
just as the underlying U.S. deficit is now expanding
(because of “AI”/ data center investment and a big
fiscal deficit).***
The easiest way to see this is to look at Chinese trade
in manufactures excluding chips and excluding gold
(refined gold is classified as a manufacture, the
commodity is gold rich ores).
Exports are up. This shouldn’t be a surprise: auto
exports are absolutely booming, and imports are still

flat.
This isn’t to say that chips aren’t important—China
runs a deficit there, and that deficit expanded just a
bit.
But the main driver of the increase in China’s
reported manufactured imports was the rise in chip

### Page 5

5
prices, which has clearly been huge, and that factor
also inflates exports.
And all this is more dramatic when examined in
higher frequency numbers. Without the surge in gold
imports the overall surplus would have been up
significantly in the second quarter even with the (big)
rise in chip prices.

### Page 6

6
There is a secondary point here.
Chinese import and export prices didn’t move much
in the first quarter, and then jumped in the second
quarter. China’s statistical authorities were slow to
recognize the “chip” shock and the broad impact of
higher memory chip prices in particular.

### Page 7

7
This did end up distorting China’s import and export
volume data in the first quarter (if the dollar value of
trade rises and import and export prices don’t rise,
statistically there is an increase in the volume of
trade).
This reduced China’s reported Q1 growth (the net
export contribution should have been bigger). And
because gold is in the Q2 data in a big way (offsetting
the fall in oil imports), true import in Q2 were likely
overstated… and that pulled down reported growth a

bit as well.
All these adjustments though are complex. The
easiest way to see what is happening is just to focus on
the manufacturing surplus without gold and chips. It
keeps on rising.
In fact, without the 1.5 pp of GDP in gold imports in
q2, the goods surplus would be close to 7% of GDP --

### Page 8

8
an absolutely massive number for an economy as big
as China.
The trend is clear. Too bad the financial press missed
it.****
*The NX contribution, which is the change in the
“real” trade surplus, was +0.8 pp of GDP in Q1 and
+0.9 pp of GDP in Q2.

### Page 9

9
** The composition of China’s imports has evolved
rapidly in the last few months. The chip price surge
has had a huge impact. So I was expecting it to have a
bigger impact on the “net” than what actually shows
up in the numbers. This is a case where rigorous
analysis that decomposes both exports and imports is
needed.

### Page 10

10
This work represents the views solely of the author(s). The
Council on Foreign Relations is an independent,
nonpartisan membership organization, think tank, and
publisher, and takes no institutional positions on matters
of policy.

This work is licensed under Creative Commons Attribution-
NonCommercial-NoDerivatives 4.0 International (CC BY-NC-
ND 4.0) License.

CHINA ECONOMICS TRADE
*** The 2025 U.S. trade data also needs to be gold-
adjusted, by the way. The threat of tariffs had a huge
impact on the Q1 data, and reversal of the flood of
imported gold bars (which are in manufactures…)
was a big reason for the relatively low U.S. trade
deficit in Q4 and Q1.
**** The rapid increase in auto export volumes, both
globally and with respect to Europe, should have
given the Economist pause before it embraced the

arguments of Adam Wolfe of Absolute Strategy
Research. Too many China analysts are trying to
obscure what should be obvious: China continues to
rely heavily on exports for growth.

### Page 11

2 Marion Laboure, Camilla Siazon, Luke Templeman, Adrian Cox, Helen Beloposky, Miha Hribernik, Jim Reid | Macro, Markets, Geopolitics and Tech: A Guide for CFOs and Corporates | 13 July 2026
Introduction: Macro, Markets, Geopolitics & T ech
A guide for CFOs & Corporates
We're pleased to share our July 2026 Guide for CFOs and

Corporates, providing essential insights into macroeconomics,
markets, geopolitics and technology. This report offers critical
analysis and perspectives to help corporate clients navigate today's
complex economic environment.
Key highlights:
— Macroeconomic outlook: Resumption of US-Iran attacks in the
Strait has reignited fears of an oil-driven energy shock. So far, the
global economy has proved to be resilient.
— Market analysis: Corporate earnings growth is expected to be

strong and credit spreads have proved resilient to several shocks.
Brent crude averages $80/bbl in 2026 ($75 in Q3).
— Geopolitics: Corporates continue to cite geopolitics as a key risk,
although other ‘normal’ risks are high priorities. Q3/Q4 events to
watch: expiry of US global 10% Section 122 tariffs in Jul,
expiration of original US-Iran ceasefire in Aug, US midterms in
Nov.
— Artificial Intelligence: AI adoption is increasing, but corporates

cite challenges in scaling across business functions. Disruption
worries are relatively latent. The costs around AI capex demand
are shifting into the spotlight.
— Monetary policy: The energy shock is influencing central bank
trajectories, with both the Fed and ECB expected to hike rates.
Momentum around policymakers considering tokenization of
assets is growing.
Source: The House View: Infographic - 2026 Global Macro Events Calendar – 06-Jan-2026

Upcoming key macro events for rest of 2026

6 Marion Laboure, Camilla Siazon, Luke Templeman, Adrian Cox, Helen Beloposky, Miha Hribernik, Jim Reid | Macro, Markets, Geopolitics and Tech: A Guide for CFOs and Corporates | 13 July 2026
Three key risks for the global economy
Collapse of the Iran ceasefire is renewing stagflation concerns, while cybersecurity risks have become a

key concern for corporates
2. AI disruption
— Concerns that AI will disrupt the labor force have yet to be
borne out by the data. CEOs see little impact on jobs in the
near term, while companies struggle to diffuse adoption.
— Central banks are becoming more attuned to AI as a source
of upwards price pressure.
1. Collapse of Iran ceasefire
— Renewed flare-ups in the Middle East are driving oil prices
higher again, compounded by energy infrastructure attacks

between Russia and Ukraine. Corporates remain concerned
with economic conditions vs six months ago.
— Long term, however, the global economy seems more
resilient to geopolitical shocks.
3. Hawkish central bank trajectories
— Expectations for higher US rates will grow as markets absorb
the apparent demise of the ceasefire. Rates expectations had
already decoupled from oil prices even during the ceasefire.
— Expectations for ECB rate hikes have also shifted due to the

energy shock.

9 Marion Laboure, Camilla Siazon, Luke Templeman, Adrian Cox, Helen Beloposky, Miha Hribernik, Jim Reid | Macro, Markets, Geopolitics and Tech: A Guide for CFOs and Corporates | 13 July 2026
Midterm Moderation: Will Trump Turn Dove?
Trump has a strong incentive not to shock markets or stoke inflation ahead of the vote in
November. But markets should expect more policy volatility, not less, as we move into

the second half of Trump’s term. An administration looking to demonstrate strength
through quick wins may pursue other issues more aggressively. Investors should watch
rising US pressure on Cuba and the political relationship with Europe as possible sources
of nearer-term movement.
The July expiry of Section 122 tariffs and their likely replacement by Section 301 levies
on dozens of trading partners should keep pressure on countries to strike bilateral deals

favourable to the US, spanning trade, investment, industrial and technology concessions.
In addition, Section 232 tariffs could expand into new categories.
While EU-US trade relations are more stable, there remains a tail risk that a transatlantic
security or sovereignty dispute (e.g. renewed American claims on Greenland) spills over
into the economic relationship. At the 7-8 July NATO summit, Trump reinforced his
expectations that European allies carry more of the security burden and his

disappointment in their lack of support for US efforts in Iran. Trump also reiterated his
continued interest in acquiring Greenland.
Trump will likely renew his push for a ceasefire in the Russia-Ukraine war. In early July,
Trump suggested “we’re getting much closer than people realize.” While a ceasefire this
year is far from guaranteed, any deal brokered by the Trump administration could offer
Russia sanctions relief, access to frozen assets and an expectation of an accelerated

Ukrainian accession to the EU.
What happens after the midterms actually matters more. Trump may become a “foreign
policy president” ,focused on cementing his legacy. This will include: embedding existing
tariff and investment deals with key US partners; deepening US leadership in the
Americas; creating the conditions for a pull-back of American resources from Europe;
and broadening US efforts to reduce rare-earths and critical-minerals dependence on

China.
Source: Library of Congress on Unsplash

10 Marion Laboure, Camilla Siazon, Luke Templeman, Adrian Cox, Helen Beloposky, Miha Hribernik, Jim Reid | Macro, Markets, Geopolitics and Tech: A Guide for CFOs and Corporates | 13 July 2026
Other geopolitical flashpoints
Key topics to monitor during 2026-Q3
— Iran: The 60-day limit for US-Iranian MOU (focusing on Iran’s nuclear programme and deal

implementation) will expire on 20 AUG but negotiations will almost certainly run past the initial
60 days. A significant escalation on over the past week saw the US launch strikes on hundreds
of targets across Iran and reimpose sanctions on Iranian oil sales in retaliation for Iran striking
ships in the Strait of Hormuz. Iran has fired a number of waves of strikes against Gulf nations.
While Trump has suggested that the ceasefire is over, we retain our assessment that both

parties are incentivized to find a near-term resolution as kinetic attacks will not drive a
resolution. That said, we should prepare for more volatility in the coming months and a
persistent threat to the Strait of Hormuz. The Middle East will likely remain a focus for the rest
of Trump’sterm, even if markets move on.
— China-US Relations: Post-summit strategic stability could deliver tactical trade and investment
gains and slow the pace of escalation into late 2026. But corporates and investors should still

prepare for a start-stop cycle of engagement and restriction as technology, tariffs and
economic security remain central to national security agendas in Beijing and Washington. A key
watchpoint will be Xi’s expected visit to the US on 24 September, just over a month before the
US midterm election set to be dominated by voter cost-of-living concerns. This could
incentivize the Trump administration to seek an economic agreement at the summit. An
extension of last October’s Busan agreement and an operationalization of the potentially

commerce-boosting US-China Board of Trade – which China hopes could include a ten-fold
increase in tariff-free trade – are possible deliverables.
— What next for Europe? An unresolved war in Ukraine, greater geopolitical fragmentation, and a less
predictable transatlantic relationship. At the same time, the geopolitical landscape is presenting myriad
opportunities – increased defence investment and a chance to build more strategic autonomy. There is

a growing recognition that the EU needs to overcome its often slow, consensus-driven decision-making
processes to respond more effectively to evolving geopolitical dynamics. The key question will be
whether Europe can convert these pressures into coordinated action, or whether internal divisions
stymie real progress.
For more on geopolitical flashpoints to watch out for, read our recent publications here: Geopolitical Outlook 2026: At the Midpoint, This Month in Geopolitics: July 2026,

Geopolitics beyond Iran, The Dragon and the Eagle: Decoding China-US Ties After Beijing
Source: IStock ugurhan

Commodity markets
Oil was trading around $73/bbl, but prices are now rising back towards $80/bbl. Several transport-
and materials-exposed companies have pointed to 2027 for normalization (see here).

Oil: Under the ceasefire, we had placed Brent at $75/bbl
(Q3’26) and $70/bbl (Q4’26).
— Oil prices fell faster than expected during the MOU-interim,
although outlook is uncertain for now with the reignition of Strait
conflict.
— Crisis has revealed need for broader reserves beyond crude oil
and gas, where disruption can rapidly cascade through supply
chains and consumer prices (i.e. refined fuels, petrochemicals,
fertilizers, food, metals).
— Companies should now be expected to maintain larger

inventories, build in more redundancy, spend more on insurance,
hedging, and transport, and accept duplicated routes and
suppliers.
Gold: revised base case to reach $4,800/oz in Q4
— Tighter Fed monetary policy and resilient US macro data has
pushed gold prices lower.
Brent crude oil prices ($/bbl) have gone from around $100/bbl to just $73/bbl during MOU

14 Marion Laboure, Camilla Siazon, Luke Templeman, Adrian Cox, Helen Beloposky, Miha Hribernik, Jim Reid | Macro, Markets, Geopolitics and Tech: A Guide for CFOs and Corporates | 13 July 2026
Rates & Credit markets
Despite higher rates, financial conditions are relatively accommodative, with credit spreads remaining
tighter than expected. Potential area for concern lies in private credit

Private credit is pricing in more stress now that full Q1 data is
in. There is potential for greater AI-disruption stress on
software and other adjacent subsectors.
— Software spreads are near Covid (March 2020) levels, but ex-
software spreads are trading much better. Market is pricing
software as an isolated issue, although there is a risk the stress will
spread more broadly over the next 6-12 months.
— Credit-spread forecasts slightly lower throughout 2026, with $IG

82bps; $HY 305bps. €IG 95bps; €HY 345bps.
Raised our YE-2026 targets for the 10yr US Treasury (4.80%)
and 10yr Bund (3.20%)
— Rationale: Expectations of more Fed hikes are now baked into our
forecast, while front loading of ECB hikes in response to the energy
shock has pushed our yield forecasts higher across the curve.
— Downside risks: AI tails from either a bubble or significant impact on
the market, or a concrete end to the Iran War leading to materially

lower oil prices.
— Upside risks: Demand for additional fiscal and capex globally or a
waning in foreign demand for US Treasuries that further lifts term
premia.
Reports: Global Fixed Income Weekly: Strategy Update - Deutsche Bank Research and Fixed Income Blog: EUR Rates Forecast
revision - Bund to 3.2% - Deutsche Bank Research and IG & HY Strategy: Global Default Monitor: Software woes, BDC selling
putting pressure on Loans - Deutsche Bank Research Source: Deutsche Bank Research, Bloomberg Finance LP.

Outlook until year-end calls for more dispersion and some spread widening
The ten year treasury yield initially traded in a tight correlation with oil prices, but that’s
completely opened up in the last month. Yields have stayed high even during the
ceasefire

15 Marion Laboure, Camilla Siazon, Luke Templeman, Adrian Cox, Helen Beloposky, Miha Hribernik, Jim Reid | Macro, Markets, Geopolitics and Tech: A Guide for CFOs and Corporates | 13 July 2026
Foreign exchange markets
Hawkish FOMC is supporting the dollar
Hard to argue for significant dollar weakness
— The dollar has modestly benefited from rising energy prices, while

equity-led inflows into the US have also started to recover.
— Near-term, the second-order effects of the Iran War is dollar
supportive.
— However, the level of front-end US Treasury yields, the interest-
rate differential with the rest of the world and relative shape of the
US yield curve are not sending dollar bullish signals.
Currency outlook
— EUR/USD: 1.20 by YE (prevs 1.25). Escalation in Iran and more
hawkish repricing of the Fed could push EUR/USD below 1.15.

— EUR/JPY: Hawkish signals from BoJ officials, firm recent data, and
potential for repatriation sees the yen rising against the Euro.
Despite yen relatively flat against the Euro since our May blueprint,
we still expect the yen to strengthen in H2, with two potential
avenues: either the BoJ hikes rates to 2% more quickly than
expected, or the government undertakes a more concerted effort to
shift domestic investment back onshore.
— USD/CNH: Renminbi's appreciation will continue. Domestic

reflation, strong real-GDP growth, surpluses, and a greater use of
RMB in cross-border transactions/internationalisation should all act
as significant tailwinds.
Currency movements and DB forecasts for YE-2026 and 2027

Artificial intelligence
Companies are committing growing sums of capital to strengthen their AI capabilities
2025 was a breakout year for corporate AI investment

— Global AI corporate investment hit $582bn in 2025,
+130% from 2024, mostly driven by Gen AI.
(Source: McKinsey/Stanford).
— Private financing (VC/PE) was the largest share of
activity.
— The US continues to lead private AI investment;
China’s true total AI spending is understated, as
government guidance funds deploy a significant
amount.
The costs for AI are growing
— AI company revenues are rising at record pace –
but so are compute and infrastructure costs.

— Frontier-model token costs specifically have
become exponentially higher than still very
intelligent mid-tier models. This is one of the
biggest challenges for wide enterprise adoption.
— There is also evidence the AI-capex demand shock
is feeding into inflation, most visibly in computer
and semiconductor prices, specifically memory.
Global corporate investment in AI
The cost of tokens for frontier models is exponentially higher than still very intelligent mid-tier

models. This is one of the biggest challenges for widespread company adoption.

18 Marion Laboure, Camilla Siazon, Luke Templeman, Adrian Cox, Helen Beloposky, Miha Hribernik, Jim Reid | Macro, Markets, Geopolitics and Tech: A Guide for CFOs and Corporates | 13 July 2026
Artificial intelligence
Job displacement from AI does not seem to be a near-term (~2 years) concern
Scaling AI adoption remains a challenge

— 88% of firms now use AI in at least one business function
(78% in 2024). Source: McKinsey/Stanford AI index
— Biggest y/y gains: Europe (+11pp to 91%) and China
(+13pp to 88%) (McKinsey/Stanford).
— Usage is highest in knowledge management for business,
legal, and professional services (58%), software
engineering (58%), and IT (56%).
— But scaling adoption lags: <10% of firms are scaling AI
agents across their firm.
AI job displacement not a near-term concern

— 56% of CEOs expect AI to have only a moderate impact
on their industries (Business Council and Conference
Board).
— Evidence so far shows little relationship between US
labour-market churn and AI adoption rate (see here).
— Yet, a third of firms expect AI to cut headcount in the
coming year – larger firms more so. The largest share
(43%) still expect little or no change. McKinsey/Stanford
Organizations are beginning to experiment with agentic AI, but diffusion is not yet

widespread
Majority of CEOs don’t think AI will fundamentally transform their firms, but most also
anticipate upskilling their workforce in the short term
Source: Deutsche Bank Research, McKinsey/Stanford Artificial Intelligence Index. Number of respondents = 1,933
Source: Deutsche Bank Research, The Conference Board Measure of CEO Confidence in collaboration with the Business Council, Q22026.
Left hand chart: Minimal impact (very few business processes will change; the industry remains largely unchanged); moderate impact (many

business processes will change, but the industry will not fundamentally transform); Significant impact (AI will affect nearlyevery business
process the industry will be fundamentally transformed.)

Asset Tokenization
— Transferable tokenized assets on-chain total $33bn (rwa.xyz).
— The SEC is  reportedly moving to permit trading of tokenized

stocks; ongoing DTCC progress on tokenized collateral
infrastructure.
— Several US banks have announced a tokenized-deposits clearing
network operated by The Clearing House, live from 2027 onwards,
with settlement in central bank reserves.
Stablecoins
— Adjusted stablecoin transaction volume is projected to reach
$719trn by 2035 (Chainalysis).
— Stablecoin payment volumes  could match Visa and Mastercard’s
off-chain transactions sometime between 2029-31.

CBDCs
— Wholesale: ECB’s Project Pontes – a settlement solution for DLT-
based transactions in central bank money – tracking for Sep 2026
launch.
— Retail: EP committee approved the digital euro framework June 23,
final law targeted end-2026. Pilot mid-2027; first issuance possible
2029.

ソニーフィナンシャルグループ
イラン後も続く円安の正体

### Page 3

2026年7月号
中東では米国とイランの武力衝突が断続的に生じているにも
拘らず、両国による覚書と60日間の停戦合意を機に 、原油価
格は下落傾向を辿っている 。日本に影響の大きいドバイ先物
価格は65 ドル／バレルを割り込み、 中東情勢の緊迫化前で
ある本年2月初の水準に戻った。弊社も含めて、本年の第3四
半期には原油価格がもとに戻るとの見方が多かったことを考
えても、やや早い動きだ。
だからと言って、日本経済にとって、エネルギーショックによる
物価への影響が直ちに収束するわけではない。
今回、宮嶋エコノミストが 4ページで整理しているように 、米国
とイランの政治的な合意の下でもホルムズ海峡の円滑な航
行が短期間で実現する保証はない 。これまでは、湾内に滞留
していた船舶の航行再開が中心であり 、ホルムズ湾岸の原
油の生産・搬出のための施設の修復も含めて 、原油供給が3
月以前の水準を回復するには相応に時間がかかる 。さらに、
日本では、石油製品の代替調達やサプライチェーンの変更に
伴うコスト増も継続するほか、それらが消費の対象となる最終

財の価格に波及するには時間的ラグもある。
それでも 、原油価格が再び大きく反発することはないとすれ
ば、エネルギーショックの 「火元」 は鎮圧されたことになり 、
我々は、過去のショックの影響が時間をかけて弱まっていくの
を見守ることになる 。そして 、このような展開になれば、 日銀
の追加利上げは、 いくつかの点から疑問が生ずることになる 。
このうち、最もシンプルな理由は実際のインフレ率が減速する
ことである。こうした視点からの疑問は、 今後の国会で議論さ
れる食料品や外食に対する時限的な消費税の減免措置に
よって、一層拍車がかかることが想定される。
また、より丁寧な議論に基づく疑問は、 金融政策の効果の波
及には「長くて不確実な」時間的ラグがある以上、 いま利上げ
しても、効果が出現する頃にはインフレ率は既に低下しており 、
利上げには意味がないだけでなく 、経済活動を過度に抑制す
るとの懸念である。
もう一つ、日銀にとっては 、リスク評価の再検討も必要になる 。
日銀は6 月の金融政策決定会合で利上げに踏み切った際に

は、基調的物価の上昇率が 2％目標を超えて上振れるリスク
を指摘した 。その理由として 、エネルギー価格上昇の消費者
段階における幅広い品目の価格上昇に繋がる可能性を挙げ
ていた。
先にみたように 、こうした現象自体は生ずるとしても 、「火元」
がなくなる以上、やがて波及は沈静化する。つまり、基調的物
価が２％目標を時限的に上回る
リスクはあるとしても 、２％目
標を超えて上振れていく リスクを主張し続けることは難しくな
る。しかも、7月の金融政策決定会合の際に改定する 「展望レ
ポート」において 、2026年度中のインフレ率見通しを4 月時点
より上方修正するようであれば 、これまでリスクと扱われてい
た内容が中心見通しに反映される結果 、残るリスクの程度は
小さくなるはずである。
つまり、現在の原油価格が本当にポスト ・エネルギーショック
の始まりを示唆する場合 、その下でも日銀が利上げを継続し
ようとすれば 、物価上昇に対するリスクマネジメント的な理由
を持ち出すことはもはや不適切になる 。その結果 、日銀は、

「物価と経済が見通しに沿って推移する限り 、金融緩和の度
合いを調整する」という元々のロジックを再び全面に押し出す
ことが必要になる。
筆者は、現在の政策金利が日銀執行部による中立金利の推
計レンジの下限にようやく達する段階にあるほか、 少なくとも
現時点で既往の利上げに伴う企業や家計、 金融市場への深
刻な影響が生じていない点を踏まえると 、日銀がさらなる利
上げを行うことには十分な合理性が存在すると考える。
その上で、利上げの理由が金融緩和度合いの調整だけに回
帰する以上、日銀は、追加利上げをしても、依然として緩和的
な金融環境が維持されることを 、従来以上に丁寧に説明し 、
企業や家計、金融市場の納得感を得ることが求められる。
この点に関して 、日銀執行部のレポート （2026-J-5）は、金融
環境の評価軸を金利動向と資金調達動向の二つに整理し 、
前者に関しては名目と実質の双方の金利動向、 後者に関し
ては資金調達コストやアベイラビリティ 、資産価格、資金調達
量を例示している 。また、日本と同じく銀行中心の金融システ

ムの下にある欧州中央銀行の政策反応関数を踏まえると 、
銀行貸出を取り巻く環境（ 預貸利ザヤ、貸出姿勢や借入需要、
担保価値）なども含めることが考えられる。
これらの要素に関する指標や分析の多くは、 既に 「展望レ
ポート」の中に盛り込まれているとはいえ 、様々に分散してい
る面もある 。金融緩和度合いの判断をより強くアピールする
上では、声明文の付属資料として一覧性のあるかたちで示す
といった工夫も有用となる。

■図表1：インフレはコストプッシュからディマンドプルへ
出所：BEA、SFGI
■図表2：米長期金利は4％台後半への上昇を見込む
注：長期金利＝期待政策金利＋タームプレミアム。期待政策金利は政策金利
見通しを表し、タームプレミアムは国債需給や国債保有リスクを反映する
出所：Bloomberg、SFGI
■図表3：業績の裏付けある株高、大幅下落のリスクは限定的
注：2024年1月を起点とする株価上昇率＝EPS上昇率＋PER上昇率
出所：Bloomberg、SFGI
3月以降、イラン情勢の緊迫化に伴う原油高がインフレ懸念を高
め、消費者マインドを下押ししてきた 。6月17日に米国とイランが
戦闘終結の覚書を結ぶと 、WTI原油は1バレル＝70 ドルを割った
が、ガソリン価格は高値在庫の影響で高止まりしており 、消費者
マインドの回復は途上にある 。一方、 この間の実体経済は底堅
い。雇用者数や低所得層の消費には弱さもあるが、 新規失業保
険申請件数や失業率は低位にあり 、求人倍率は1 倍台を回復し

ている。実質GDP成長率は1-3月期に前期比年率2.1％と潜在成
長率を上回ったが、 4-6月期は在庫積み増しもあって一段の加
速が見込まれる 。背景には、①AI関連設備投資の活況、 ②株高
による資産効果 、③大型減税・歳出法の効果 、④対米直接投資
の流入などがある。
物価をみると、コアPCEデフレーターは再加速している（図表1）。ト
ランプ関税や中東情勢に伴う原油高がコア財価格を押し上げたた
めだ。ただし、関税の影響は一巡しつつあるほか、原油高の影響
も年内のピークアウトが見込まれる。長期期待インフレ率（インフレ
スワップ5年先5年など）は原油価格の変動にもかかわらず安定し
ており、賃金やサービス物価への二次的波及の兆候は見えない。
もっとも、当社はインフレ率が来年以降も2％目標に着地しないと
みている。堅調な景気と需給ギャップの拡大がディマンドプル・ イ
ンフレを招くためだ 。コアPCEデフレータは年内は 3％台、来年も
2％台半ばでの高止まりを想定する 。この前提の下、金融政策は

年内は据え置き、来年は2回（6・12月）の利上げを見込む。一方、
金利先物市場は年内に1.4回の利上げを織り込んでいる 。FRBの
ウォーシュ議長がインフレ抑制を重視していることから、市場は将
来の物価上振れを回避する予防的利上げを想定している模様だ 。
仮に、原油価格の下落などで早期利上げ観測が後退すれば、 金
融市場では金利低下・ドル安・株高が進む可能性がある。
米長期金利は現在4 .5％近辺で高止まりしている （図表2）。昨年
来低下してきた期待政策金利（ 政策金利予想）は、イラン情勢に
伴うインフレ懸念や米景気の堅調、 FRBのタカ派化などを背景に
上昇に転じた。一方、高止まりしていたタームプレミアム （国債保
有リスク ）は和平交渉を受けて足元では低下している 。だが、中
間選挙に向けて拡張財政が加速すれば、 再び上昇しやすい 。さ
らに、ウォーシュFRB議長が量的引き締め （QT）を進める可能性
もある。不確実性や国債需給の悪化懸念は、長期金利の変動幅
を拡大させるとともに、上昇リスクを高めよう。

一方、株価は上昇継続とみる。AI・半導体株には過熱感も指摘さ
れるが、足元までの株高は企業業績（ EPS）の拡大が主因であり 、
市場心理（PER）の高揚だけによるものではない（図表3）。短期的
には株価急落はあり得るが、業績の裏付けがある分、大きく崩れ
るリスクは低いだろう。当社はAIの進展による生産性向上がEPS
を押し上げ、成長期待がPERを支えることで、年末のS&P500指数
は8000ポイント超を見込む。リスクは長期金利の上振れだ 。それ
はPERだけでなく、投資抑制を通じてEPSも押し下げる。当面はAI
期待の持続性と金利上昇リスクが株価の方向性を左右しよう。
実体経済は堅調、原油下落などで早期利上げ期待が後退すれば債券高・株高も
長期金利はインフレ懸念と国債需給懸念から上昇の公算、株式市場はAI期待が牽引

日本10年債利回り（左軸）
米国10年債利回り（右軸）
日本経済見通し
4
6月の日本の長期金利は変動の大きい展開となったが、 月末値

は月初値からほぼ横ばいとなった （図表1）。6月初めの長期金利
は植田日銀総裁の講演におけるタカ派的発言が意識されて上昇
した。中旬になると 、米国とイランの和平協議進展や日銀6 月会
合での国債買入れ減額停止観測を受けて、長期金利は下落に転
じた。その後、長期金利は再び上昇し始めた。米国の利上げ観測
が高まったことに加え 、6月会合の内田副総裁会見などからビハ
インドザカーブ懸念が再び意識されたことが挙げられる。
6月会合で日本銀行は今後のインフレリスクが大きいとの認識を
示したが、一方で利上げペースの前倒しや利上げ幅拡大の可能
性は示唆しなかった。債券市場では日銀のビハインドザカーブ懸
念が高まっているものの、 日銀は2026年度後半から27年度での
物価目標達成を予想し、強い懸念を示していない。中東情勢が最
悪期を過ぎたとみられることや、長期インフレ予想が2％に完全に
アンカーされたわけではなく上振れだけでなく下振れリスクもある
こと、今回の利上げで政策金利水準が中立金利の推計値下限付

近に到達したことなどから 、日本銀行は次回利上げのタイミング
を慎重に見極めていくと予想される。
基調的物価を見通すことは困難な状況だ 。中東情勢の先行きは
依然不透明である点や、仮に和平協議が進んだとしても、ホルム
ズ海峡の完全な正常化には時間を要すると予想されること、石油
製品の代替調達やサプライチェーンの変更に伴いコスト高は続く
こと、円安地合いが強まっていることなどから 、インフレが予想よ
りも長引くリスクは否定できない。一方、高市政権による補助金政
策によりコア CPIの押し下げ効果が続くことや、 来月にはCPIの5
年に1度の基準改定により過去分の遡及改定が予定されている
こと、やや先の話になるが食品の消費税率引き下げやそれに伴
う外食などへの補助金がCPIを下押しするとみられることなどから、
インフレの基調が読みにくくなり 、加えてコアCPI実績値の下振れ
を受けてインフレ予想も低下するという下方リスクもある。こうした
点も踏まえ 、日本銀行は金融環境の状況を見極めながら 、次の

利上げ時期を慎重に探っていく姿勢とみられる。当社メインシナリ
オでは引き続き次の利上げ時期を12月と予想する。ただし、為替
レートが170円/ドルに近づくような急速な円安が起これば 、利上
げ時期が前倒しされるリスクがある点には留意されたい。
日本の景気下振れリスクは低下している。石油備蓄日数をみると、
6月はわずか▲4日分の減少と、5月（▲6日分）に続いて小幅にと
どまった。もちろんミクロでみれば供給制約が依然厳しい分野も
あると推察されるが、 石油枯渇による景気後退という最悪のリス
クは後退したとみられる 。日銀短観6
月調査では、企業の景況感
は予想以上の改善、設備投資計画にも大きな変調はみられない 。
企業の値上げ意向は引き続き高く（ 図表2）、資金繰りなどを見る
限り、金融環境も引き続き緩和的だ（図表3）。4～5月の日銀消費
活動指数も高い伸び率であり 、特にサービス消費の増加が続い
ている点は明るい材料だ。株高などの資産効果もあり、推し活・イ
ベント需要などは引き続き旺盛とみられる。

再び高まる市場でのビハインドザカーブ懸念、一方で日銀は次回利上げ時期を見極めへ
日銀短観6月調査は強い結果、日本の景気後退リスクは低下

2026年7月号
■図表1：ユーロ圏GDPは特殊要因を除いても弱含み
出所：EUROSTAT、SFGI
■図表2：ECBはインフレの顕在化に対応し、6月に利上げ
注：物価予想（点線部分）はECBスタッフが6月理事会で示した4つのシナリオ
のうちの「基本」と「穏健」。「逆風」「深刻」は割愛
出所：EUROSTAT、ECB、SFGI
■図表3：長期インフレ期待は引き続き安定
注：市場の長期インフレ期待は、インフレスワップ5年先5年
出所： ECB（Survey of Professional Forecaster)、Bloomberg、SFGI
ユーロ圏経済は低迷している 。2026年1-3月期の実質GDP 成長
率は前期比年率▲ 0.9％となった 。その主因は多国籍企業の活
動に左右されやすいアイルランドの特殊要因だが 、これを除いて
も1％近辺の成長にとどまっている （図表1）。国別ではドイツが相
対的に堅調で 、防衛費拡大やインフラ投資を中心とした財政支
出の増加が下支えとなった 。イタリア、スペインなどの南欧経済

は、人口増や観光ブームが成長を押し上げている 。一方、フラン
スでは政治的不確実性の高まりを背景に家計・ 企業マインドや
内需が弱含み、成長率はマイナス圏に沈んだ 。需要面では設備
投資が高金利環境の下で慎重化し、米国向けを中心に輸出も低
迷している。生産面では、イラン情勢に伴うエネルギー価格の上
昇がサービス業の重荷となっている 。先行きも停滞色の強い展
開が見込まれる。米国につられた域内の高金利が企業の投資を
抑制することや 、ユーロ高による輸出競争力の低下 、中国の需
要低迷などの逆風がある。また、貸出態度の厳格化や資金需要
の減少といった金融環境の引き締まりが警戒されるほか、 各地
を襲う熱波が生産 ・消費活動を下押しする反面 、食料や電力価
格を押し上げる可能性にも留意が必要だ。
金融政策では、物価上昇を背景に、ECBは6月理事会で約3年ぶ
りの利上げを決め 、預金ファシリティ金利を2 .25％へ引き上げた
（図表2）。ECBは今回の利上げについて 、将来のインフレリスク
に対する 「予防的」「保険的」な措置ではなく、 顕在化しているエ

ネルギーショックへの対応であることを強調した 。同時に発表し
た経済・ 物価見通しでは、 基本・穏健・逆風・深刻の4 つのシナリ
オを提示しつつ、来年以降の物価が2％目標を下回る穏健シナリ
オの下でも今回の利上げが正当化されることを強調した 。もっと
も、6月のインフレ率は5月よりも低下し、穏健シナリオの想定をも
下回った。また、ラガルドECB総裁も認めるように 、長期期待イン
フレ率は、2％物価目標と整合的な水準で安定している （図表3）。
現時点では、エネルギー価格上昇が賃金とサービス価格の持続
的な上昇につながる 、いわゆる二次的波及は限定的とみられる 。
そのためECBは、連続利上げを前提とした機械的な政策運営を
否定し、会合ごとに公表されるデータを評価しながら政策を判断
する方針だ 。また、6月末にシントラで開催された ECBフォーラム
においてラガルド総裁は 、不確実性が高い環境下ではあらかじ
め決められた金利経路は存在しないと改めて強調し 、フォワード
ガイダンスについても 、低インフレ時代に有効な政策手段であり 、

今後はこれに依存しない政策運営を志向する考えを示した。
なお、現在の預金ファシリティ金利2 .25％は、ECBが推計する中
立金利レンジ（1.75～2.25％）の上限に位置する。ここからの追加
利上げは実質的に引き締め領域への踏み込みを意味する。市場
では年内にあと1回弱の利上げが織り込まれているが、当社はエ
ネルギーインフレの年内ピークアウトと来年中の2 ％目標への収
束を想定し、政策は据え置きと予想する。経済成長率の低迷に加
え、金融環境が引き締まるリスクも踏まえ、金融政策は景気の下
振れや信用収縮のリスクへの配慮が必要な局面にあるといえる 。
ユーロ圏景気は低迷、先行きは金融環境の変化や熱波の影響に警戒
ECBは利上げを決定、インフレやインフレ期待は安定、金融政策は当面据え置きの公算

■図表1：6月の中国株は香港・本土市場で明暗が分かれる
出所：Bloomberg
■図表2：小売売上高はコロナ禍を除けば記録的な低水準
出所：中国国家統計局、Macrobond
■図表3：ハイテク輸出が景気のけん引役に
出所：中国海関総署、Macrobond

91 92 94 95 97 98 00 02 03 05 06 08 10 11 13 14 16 17 19 21 22 24 25
（年）
小売売上高
（前年比％）
中国経済見通し
6
6月の中国株のうち 、香港ハンセン中国企業指数は大幅下落、
CSI300指数は小幅に上昇した （図表1）。中東情勢の改善はプラ
ス要因となったものの、香港市場では内需悪化への懸念や当局
による違法な越境証券取引の取り締まり強化が嫌気された 。一

方、本土市場では政府による AIデータセンター建設への大型投
資計画や最先端技術開発加速方針が好感された。
中国経済は現在、輸出好調の一方で内需低迷という大きな構造
的矛盾に直面している 。5月の小売売上高はゼロコロナ政策解
除直後の2022 年12月以来初めて前年割れ （図表2）となり、不動
産不況を背景に固定資産投資も減少幅が拡大した 。一方で、半
導体などのハイテク品やEV、再生可能エネルギー関連製品を中
心に輸出は好調（図表3）で景気を支えている。こうした輸出主導
の成長により政府が掲げる5％前後の経済成長目標は達成可能
とみられるものの、欧米との貿易摩擦は一段と強まりつつある。
輸出を牽引しているのは、 中国政府が重点分野としている ハイ
テク品目だ。AI、半導体、ロボットなどへの巨額投資が実を結び 、
中国企業は世界市場で急速に存在感を高めている 。半導体輸
出額は前年同月比で 2倍超となり 、AI投資ブームを背景にコン
ピューター・データ処理機器などの輸出も大幅に拡大した 。また、

AI分野ではDeepSeek に続き、Zhipu（智譜）が高性能かつ低コス
トの大規模言語モデルを発表し 、中国AIは性能・ 価格の両面で
米国勢との差を縮めつつあるとの見方も広がっている 。ただし、
最先端半導体の不足や計算資源の制約から 、総合的には 依然
として米国AIが優位との評価も残る。
しかし、中国の新たな成長モデルには大きな課題もある 。ハイテ
ク産業は北京、上海、深セン、杭州など一部都市に集中しており 、
省力化が進むため雇用創出効果は従来の不動産や製造業ほど
大きくない 。その結果、 多くの地方都市では人口流出や住宅価
格下落、税収減少が続き 、若年層の雇用環境も改善していない 。
地方政府はAI パークや EV 工場の建設を競うものの、 サプライ
チェーンや人材不足から失敗例も多く、空き施設や稼働停止した
工場も目立つ 。さらに 、補助金競争が企業の過剰参入を招き 、
価格競争の激化によって企業収益は悪化し、赤字企業比率は過
去最高水準に達している 。こうした 産業政策が生産性向上を促

す一方で、資源配分の歪みも生み出している面もある。
こうした輸出依存の強まりは、 中国の国際的な立場にも影響を
与えている 。欧州では中国製品への依存を減らすデリスキング
の動きが広がり 、米国もAIや半導体だけでなく EV、クラウド 、ロ
ボット企業まで軍事関連企業リストを拡大した 。これに対し、中国
もレアアース などを通じた対抗措置を打ち出し 、米中対立は技
術・安全保障分野まで 一段と広がっている 。中国の 技術レベル
は着実に向上する一方で 、消費低迷、不動産不況、地方財政悪
化、地域格差という構造問題は依然として解決されていない 。製
造業や輸出への過度な依存から脱却し 、家計消費を中心とした
内需主導型の経済へ転換 すべきだが、 米国との地政学的な競
争における勝利を最優先する政策姿勢が続くと筆者は予想する 。
不振極まる中国の内需、輸出主導の成長でカバーする構図が継続
ハイテク産業振興政策は奏功、ただし格差などの深刻な構造問題を助長

1996 1999 2002 2005 2008 2011 2014 2017 2020 2023
輸送 旅行
保険・年金 金融
デジタル赤字 その他
サービス収支
（兆円）
6月30日、ドル円は約39年ぶりとなる162円台後半の高値を付
けた。2024年の高値161円95銭を上抜けると 、ドル高・円安が
加速。ここまで来ると 、テクニカル上あまり明確な上値抵抗線
はなく、強いて言えば 1986年11月に付けた戻り高値の 164円
50銭付近が、次の上値メドとして挙げられよう 。しかし、そこも
超えると、1985年のプラザ合意以降1 ドル＝240円台から急落
してきた相場なだけに 、上値メドを予想するのは難しく、 所謂
「青天井」になりかねない環境と言えよう。
今年4月～5月に実施された円買い介入は、 約12兆円の投入
で、ドル円の押し下げ幅は約5 円だったことから 「介入は効か
ない」との見方が専らだが、 むしろよく現在の水準に留まって

いるというのが筆者の印象だ 。161円95銭を超えて 、164円付
近まで一気に円安が加速してもおかしくない環境だが 、介入
警戒感がそれを阻んでいると思われる 。また、4月は直前まで
片山大臣が 「断固たる措置 」を連呼し 、連休中の介入も匂わ
せた上で実施された 。投機筋に円売りに対する不安感を与え
るためには 、前回のように明快な手法ではなく 、覆面介入に
よってじわじわと相場に影響を与えようとするかもしれない。
しかし、介入が時間稼ぎになったとしても、円安のトレンドはな
ぜ止まらないのか。 短期的には、米国の利上げ観測の高まり
によるドル高に加え、高市政権が6月30日に公表した「骨太の
方針」の原案が影響しているようだ 。同原案に、「『強い経済』
の実現を目指しており 、そのためにも『安定的な物価上昇』 の
実現に資する適切な金融政策運営を伴うことが非常に重要 」
との記載があったことが注目された 。前段の文脈を踏まえれ
ば政府としては 「デフレに後戻りすることのない物価安定 」を

目指しているのであって、これを「日銀の利上げに対するけん
制」だと捉えるのは 、やや過剰反応と言えるが 、市場ではそ
のように解釈されている 。加えて 、今回の「骨太の方針 」は、
「強い経済の実現」のための経済運営の政策ばかりが目立ち 、
財政改革の方針は見当たらず 、「財政健全化」 の文言も消え
た。これにより、財政懸念が再び台頭し 、市場では円安ととも
に長期金利が再び上昇しはじめている 。株価もさらに下落す
れば、「トリプル安 」となるリスクを孕む 。一方、長期的な円安
要因としては、 日本の潜在成長率が低いことに加え 、実質金
利が依然としてマイナスであることから 、投資マネーが日本か
ら海外へ流出しやすいことが挙げられよう 。財務省の金融収
支を見ると、今年4月時点の海外への直接投資は2 .8兆円と、
この20年で約7倍となっている 。円安進行により 、製造業の生
産は今後一部国内に戻る可能性はあるものの、 海外に向か
う日本の投資マネーを本格的に国内回帰させる 、或いは海外

からの投資が積極的に日本に向かうことを促すには、 日本の
成長期待が高まることが大前提だ。
ところで、筆者がやや違和感を覚える のは、「デジタル赤字が
円安を促している 」という説が 、最近報じられるケースが目立
つことだ。そもそも日本の経常収支は今年 4月時点で4.2兆円
の黒字で、所謂「デジタル赤字」 はそのうちのサービス収支に
含まれる 「通信・ コンピュータ ・情報サービス 、著作権等使用
料、コンサルティング料 」などに該当し 、約6,000億円と規模も
小さい。サービス収支の中身を詳しく見てみると （図表）、確か
にデジタル赤字（ 図表・ 黄色） は拡大しつつあるものの、 イン
バウンド効果による旅行収支の黒字（ オレンジ）の拡大で、ほ
ぼ相殺される規模である 。この他、ここ数年サービス収支の
赤字を拡大させている要因として 、保険・ 年金サービス （図
表・緑）が挙げられよう 。地震、台風、豪雨などの自然災害の
増加や大型化に伴い、日本の保険会社は海外の再保険会社
にリスクを移転する傾向にあり 、これがサービスの輸入増を

促している。また、保険会社による海外資産の運用の増加は 、
海外の金融機関による 資産管理や運用委託などのサービス
の輸入増に繋がっているようだ 。AIなどの最新テクノロジーが
米国のサービスに集中していることは言うまでもないが 、日本
のIT・デジタル化の遅れを踏まえれば、 今後も利用料などデ
ジタル赤字は増加傾向となり 、いずれ無視できない規模とな
る可能性はある。しかし、これまでの円安を主にデジタル赤字
で説明するのは、やや無理があるのではないか。
為替相場展望

1カ月 3カ月 コメント
依然としてリスク許容度が拡大する局面では
高金利のブラジルレアルには買いが入りやす
い状態と見る。ただ大統領選関連の報道やエ
ルニーニョ現象に伴う経済的損失などが逆風
になる可能性も。
カナダ中銀は引き続き様子見姿勢を維持。
USMCAの交渉はまだ序盤であり、今後の波
乱要因になり得る。また、目先は森林火災の
発生と被害などにも要注意だ。
NZ中銀の「タカ派的据え置き」から5月に大きく
買われるも、原油安などを受けて、市場は過

度に織り込んでいた利上げを後退させた（年
内3回利上げ→2回強）。8日の中銀発表やそ
の後のデータ次第でさらに調整も。
市場の利上げ期待はRBAの見通し以上に後
退しているが、今後経済データなどを確認しつ
つ、市場がRBAのタカ派姿勢に歩み寄るなら
豪ドル高へ。
スターマー首相の後任と有力視されるバーナ
ム氏は財政規律を遵守する方針を示し 、ポン
ドの買い戻し圧力となっている 。ただ、中長期
的には引き締め的な金融政策が重石となり 、
英経済の悪化がポンド売り圧力に。
ECBはインフレ抑制を優先して利上げを実施
するも、ユーロ圏経済は独仏を中心に引き続
き軟調。景気悪化が鮮明となれば、 ユーロの
下押し圧力は強まる公算。

日銀、25bp利上げ
（6/16）
4月末、本邦政府・日銀による円買い・ドル売り介入によってド
ル円相場は 5月6日に2月24日以来の水準となる 155円ちょう
ど付近まで値を下げた。しかしその後はジリジリと値を戻し 、6
月18日には4月末の高値を抜けて上昇が加速 。7月1日には
1986年以来となる162円80銭を付けた。その後は日本の介入
への警戒感が燻る中で 、本稿執筆時点にかけては上値が抑
えられているが、積極的に円が買われる様子もまた乏しい。

こうしたドル円の上昇の主たる原動力は米ドル高だ。6月の米
連邦公開市場委員会（ FOMC）で示された経済見通し （SEP）
においては 、経済は潜在成長率をやや上回って推移する一
方、物価は上方修正され、 2028年にかけて高止まりするとの
見方が示された（詳細は6/18Inoue Report）。政策金利に関し
ては、FF金利の予想中央値は2026年末時点で3.75％と「据え
置き」 が示唆されたが、 ドットチャートは据え置きと利下げ予
想が計9 人、1回以上の利上げ予想が9 人と割れている状態
であり、市場はこのイベントを 「タカ派的」と評価した 。その後
は図表1・2の通り、年内の利上げ観測を強めつつ 、ドルが買
われる展開となった 。しかし、ドットチャートの分布を見るに 、
FOMCメンバーの見方のバラツキは大きいと言わざるを得な
い。また 、新たに米連邦準備理事会（ FRB）議長に就任した
ウォーシュ氏は本見通しに参加していない 。そのウォーシュ
氏は7月1日、現在の物価水準は「 依然として高すぎる 」という

認識をFRB 内で共有しているとしつつも 、中東に絡む混乱が
一定程度落ち着き 、原油価格が下がったことで 「（ここ数週間
で）インフレリスクは低下した 」と言及しており 、利上げには慎
重な様子も見受けられる 。一方、 市場は本稿執筆時点で年
末までに約1.4回程度の利上げを完全に織り込んでいる 。当
面は、この市場の織り込みが整合的か、 データを見ていく流
れとなろう。7月28‐29日のFOMCでの政策変更期待は薄いが、
現状をFOMCがどう認識しているのかが注目されよう 。弱い
経済指標が続くことがあれば 、市場が利上げ織り込みを修正
する中で強めのドル売り圧力が生じる可能性もある 。仮に
2022年・2024年時のように 、日本の円買い介入が弱い米指
標発表のタイミングに合わせて実施されれば 、ドル円相場の
水準が短期的に大きく下方へ修正されることも起こり得る。
ただし 、日本サイドの材料として 、6月末に示された政府の
「骨太の方針（原案）」を受け 、日銀の利上げが阻害されるの

では、財政健全化が後退するのでは、 との不安が広がってお
り、これが円の重石となっている。短期的なドル円の下押しが
あったとしても、じわじわと再び円売り圧力が強まる可能性は
留意すべきだろう 。米利上げについての市場の過度な織り込
みも、一旦大きな修正が入れば、再び金利先高感の中でドル
買い優勢に転じると見る 。当社は図表3のように、先月の当レ
ポートで示した見通しを維持する。
米国の金利先高感が米ドル上昇の原動力に
市場の利上げ織り込みの修正があれば短期的なドル安も

出所：ECB、Macrobond
6月中旬以降 、ユーロは対ドルで上値の重い展開となってい
る。米経済の底堅さや、 6月米連邦公開市場委員会 （FOMC）
がタカ派的との受け止め方が広がる中 、為替市場において
はドル買いが優勢に 。ユーロドルは昨年6 月以来の安値水準
まで下落した 。一方、 対円では日本の財政悪化への懸念が
円の重石となる中、高値圏での推移が続いている（図表1）。
欧州中銀（ ECB）は6月11日の理事会で 、25bpの利上げを決
定した。今回注目されたのは 、ラガルド総裁が質疑応答で示
した判断の強さだ 。同総裁は、 今回の25bp 利上げは全会一
致で決定され、 他の選択肢は議論されなかったと発言。 また、
今回の決定が 「保険的・予防的」利上げとの見方を明確に否

定し、長期化するエネルギーショックが経済全体へ広がり始
めていることを踏まえた 、通常の金融政策判断だったと強調
した。さらに、従来のadverse、severeシナリオに加え 、エネル
ギー価格の上振れが軽微にとどまる milderシナリオを新たに
用意したうえで （図表2）、どのシナリオでも利上げは正当化さ
れると述べた点は 、ECBが単なるリスク管理ではなく 、見通し
に基づく政策判断として利上げを位置付けていることを示して
いる。なお、ラガルド総裁は後日ポルトガルのシントラで行わ
れたECBフォーラムにおいても、改めて同様の見方を示した。
もっとも 、今回の利上げはやや時期尚早感もある 。ラガルド
総裁は、5月のサービスインフレ率が+ 3.5％へ上昇したことに
ついて「大幅な上昇」と認めつつも、それがエネルギー価格の
直接・間接的影響によるものなのか、 あるいは別の要因なの
かはまだ見極めが必要とした 。また、賃金を通じた本格的な
二次的波及は現時点で確認されていないとも述べている 。6

月のユーロ圏HICP は前年比+2.8％と、5月の+3.2％から低下。
エネルギー価格はなお+8.7％上昇と高いものの 、サービス価
格は+3.2％へ鈍化し 、食品・ エネルギー等を除くコア指数も
+2.4％へ低下した。加えて、ECBの5月消費者期待調査では、
1年先の期待インフレ率は+3.5％へ低下し、3年先は+2.9％、5
年先は+2.4％で横ばいとなった。milderシナリオの追加を含め、
今回の決定は「利上げありき」のようにも見える。
また、景気面ではユーロの下押し材料も多い 。ECBの6月ス
タッフ見通しでは 、2026年と2027年の成長率が下方修正され
た。6月の総合PMI は50.0まで持ち直したものの、 これは内需
の持ち直しや中東情勢を巡る供給懸念の後退を要因とした
スペインの急回復に牽引されたという面が大きく、 ユーロ圏景
気全体の本格回復を示すものとまでは言い難い （図表3）。今
回の利上げにより 、政策金利は中立金利の推計レンジの上
限付近に位置していると考えられるが、 景気の力強さを欠く

中での高金利は、 企業の設備投資や資金需要の抑制を通じ
て、ユーロ圏経済の重石となる。こうした景気下押し圧力が意
識されれば、 市場で過度に織り込まれている追加利上げ期
待は後退し、対ドルを中心にユーロ売り圧力が強まるとみる。
